Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-32 · 92
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 3293

Case S2-32 · why

1 · BAngle recession glaucoma OD

A wide band of ciliary body over four clock hours with torn iris processes is a recessed angle, and the sphincter tears and focal lens opacity date it to the injury. The fellow eye is normal.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EAsymmetrical POAGOpen angles with a notched disc and arcuate loss is its usual picture.Primary disease does not recess the ciliary band or tear iris processes.
CPseudoexfoliationIt is the commonest cause of a genuinely unilateral high pressure.No flakes on the pupil margin or capsule, and the pigment is focal.
2 · D, E, FRecessed band, torn processes, sphincter tears

Only the structural scars carry a date: a recessed ciliary band, torn processes, and a torn sphincter beside a focal lens opacity. The other findings measure severity, not mechanism.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
A, BPressure and cuppingThey are the abnormal numbers, and they are what drives the treatment.Both occur in primary disease, so neither dates the damage to 20 years ago.
CCorneal thicknessThickness corrects the pressure reading, which makes it feel diagnostic.545 µm is average, and it says nothing about why this angle is damaged.
3 · CTopical prostaglandin OD, review at four weeks

A notched disc with a matching arcuate defect needs the pressure down, and a prostaglandin gives the largest single-agent reduction. Medical therapy comes before laser or a filtering operation.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DLaser trabeculoplastyIt is a standard first line where the meshwork is open and visible.A traumatised meshwork answers laser poorly and unpredictably.
ATrabeculectomy nowEstablished field loss at 30 mmHg looks like a surgical problem.He has had no medical trial. Surgery is not the opening move here.
4 · EProgressive thinning at 24 mmHg

Escalation follows progression rather than a number. Structural loss continuing at 24 mmHg means the target is too high for this nerve, while a stable disc at the same pressure means it is not.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AStable at 24 mmHg24 sits above the usual target, so treating the number feels safer.A nerve holding steady for a year says the current target is working.
CSpike after a missed doseA reading of 30 on treatment reads as outright failure of the drop.An unmedicated pressure measures adherence, not the regimen.
5 · CBinocular field decides, advice recorded

Fitness turns on the binocular field, and a full fellow field covers this arcuate defect. He is told the position, formally field tested if the defect spreads, and the advice is recorded.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DNotify at onceThe word glaucoma sounds notifiable in and of itself.One eye affected with a full fellow field does not meet the test.
APressure under 21 mmHg21 is the number patients remember, so it feels like the threshold.Licensing is decided on field and acuity, never on a pressure reading.
If-then rules from this caseIf one eye alone runs a high pressure, gonioscope both angles before naming the disease. If a wide ciliary band is seen, expect a poor answer to trabeculoplasty and monitor for life.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-33 · 94
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 3395

Case S2-33 · why

1 · BDrug-induced ciliochoroidal effusion

Simultaneous bilateral closure, a 3.50 D myopic shift, shallowing at the centre as well as the periphery, and annular choroidal elevation place the block behind the iris. The drug started 11 days ago.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
APrimary angle closureThe pressures, the haze and the closed angles are identical to it.Primary closure takes one eye at a time and shifts no refraction.
DPhacomorphic closureA lens moving forward does shallow a chamber and close an angle.Both lenses are clear, and both eyes closed together in 36 hours.
2 · C, D, EStop the drug, aqueous suppression, atropine, steroid

The block sits behind the iris, so the drug comes out, the ciliary body is relaxed and aqueous is suppressed while the effusion settles. The diaphragm falls back over days and the angles reopen.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
FLaser iridotomyClosed angles at 40 mmHg is the textbook indication for a hole.There is no pupil block to relieve, so the angle stays shut afterwards.
A, BPilocarpine, iridoplastyBoth pull peripheral iris out of the angle in other closures.Miotics rotate the ciliary body further forward and worsen this one.
3 · AAcetazolamide and a hyperosmotic, atropine kept

Medical control buys the days the effusion needs once the drug is withdrawn. Nothing here calls for a hole in the iris or the loss of a clear lens, and both act on a mechanism that is absent.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BIridotomy nowA pressure of 34 on maximal drops reads as failure needing laser.The angle is shut by forward rotation, which an iridotomy leaves intact.
DLens extractionRemoving the lens does deepen a crowded anterior segment for good.These lenses are clear and the mechanism resolves in days regardless.
4 · DForward lens movement on a rotated ciliary body

Fluid in the supraciliary space rotates the ciliary body forward and slackens the zonule, so the lens moves anteriorly and thickens. The eye turns myopic and the chamber shallows across its width.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ELens swellingLens hydration is a real cause of acute myopia in hyperglycaemia.These lenses are clear, and swelling would not lift the choroid.
BCiliary spasmSpasm gives myopia and brow ache, and both are present here.Spasm closes no angle and produces no annular choroidal elevation.
5 · ECentral as well as peripheral shallowing

Pupil block bows the peripheral iris and leaves central depth near normal. Uniform shallowing with a myopic shift means the whole lens-iris diaphragm has come forward, which is a different disease.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
APressure above 40The height of the pressure feels like a measure of the mechanism.Both mechanisms reach the forties, so the number separates nothing.
B, COedema, closed angleThese are the findings that name an angle closure in the first place.Shared by both, and neither says what pushed the iris forward.
If-then rules from this caseIf both angles close at once with a myopic shift, stop the new drug and look behind the iris. If the chamber is shallow centrally as well as peripherally, an iridotomy will not open it.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-34 · 96
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 3497

Case S2-34 · why

1 · APlateau iris syndrome after iridotomy

Appositional closure with a rising pressure despite two patent iridotomies, a flat iris with normal central depth, a double hump on indentation, and anteriorly placed ciliary processes define plateau iris.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EResidual pupil blockClosure after a laser suggests the hole is too small or has sealed.Both holes transilluminate and the central chamber depth is normal.
BMalignant glaucomaIt too closes an angle in an eye that already has a patent iridotomy.It follows filtering surgery and shallows the chamber centrally.
2 · B, C, DPatent holes, flat iris, double hump

A patent hole removes block, and its signature, a bowed iris over a shallow centre, is absent. The double hump shows ciliary processes holding the peripheral iris against the meshwork instead.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
A, EPressure and refractionOne is the abnormal number, the other the classic closure risk factor.Neither locates the block, and +0.75 argues against a crowded eye.
FClosure in the darkIt proves the angle does close, which is the worry in front of you.It shows that closure happens, not what pushes the iris forward.
3 · AIridoplasty OS with a pressure-lowering drop

Iridoplasty contracts peripheral iris stroma and pulls it out of the angle, which is the only laser that treats this configuration. A drop holds the pressure while the angle is reopened.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BA second iridotomyAnother hole feels logical when the first one did not hold the angle.The hole is patent, so a second changes nothing about the ciliary body.
CTrabeculectomy30 mmHg after a laser that failed looks like a surgical eye.Filtration is for uncontrolled damage, not an angle that can be opened.
4 · BGonioscopy on a schedule, treat on change

This eye holds 15 mmHg with no synechiae, so it is followed with the test that shows apposition. Treatment is triggered by a pressure rise or new adhesions, not by the configuration alone.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CIridoplasty both eyesThe configuration is bilateral, so treating both looks consistent.No pressure rise and no synechiae here. There is nothing yet to treat.
ADischarge15 mmHg with 20/20 and a patent iridotomy reads as a normal eye.An angle that closes in the dark needs gonioscopy, not discharge.
5 · AWarn the prescriber, dilation can close the angle

A patent iridotomy removes pupil block but not plateau configuration, so a dilated pupil still crowds the angle. The prescriber needs that before the first tablet, not after the attack.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BNo precaution neededThe iridotomy is the treatment everyone associates with angle closure.It prevents block alone, and this angle closes without any block.
EOnly drops matterTopical drugs reach the iris directly, which sounds like the greater risk.Systemic anticholinergics dilate the pupil as effectively as a drop.
If-then rules from this caseIf an angle still closes with a patent iridotomy, a flat iris and normal central depth, the block sits at the iris root. Treat the configuration with iridoplasty, and warn about dilation.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-35 · 98
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 3599

Case S2-35 · why

1 · B20%

Of 1000 screened, 40 have disease and 36 are detected, while 144 of the 960 healthy screen positive. That makes 180 positives, of which 36 are true, so 36 divided by 180 gives 20%.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
C90%, the sensitivitySensitivity is the figure quoted for the test, so it reads as its accuracy.Sensitivity looks forward from disease, not back from a positive result.
A4%, the prevalenceIt is the other number in the stem, and the disease is genuinely rare.A positive result moves his probability from 4% up to 20%.
2 · A, BPPV rises to 60%, the test itself unchanged

At 20% prevalence, 200 of 1000 have disease and 180 are detected, while 120 of the 800 healthy screen positive. So 180 of 300 positives are true. The instrument has not changed at all.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CNPV rises to 99%A better positive value at higher prevalence sounds like it lifts both.More disease means more missed cases, so the negative value falls to 97%.
D, E200 false positivesBoth follow from treating prevalence as a property of the instrument.With 800 healthy people, false positives fall to 120, and 85% is fixed.
3 · ADocument the explanation, the risk and the refusal

A refusal is valid when a person with capacity has been told what the test is for and what untreated disease costs. What makes it informed is the record of that exchange, and an open door back.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CSigned waiverA signature looks like the strongest protection the practice can hold.Liability cannot be signed away, and a form is not a conversation.
DRefer him anywayThe clinical risk is real, and referral feels like the safe default.Acting against the refusal of a competent adult is the greater wrong.
4 · BA copy to him, inside the statutory period

The file belongs to the practice and the information in it belongs to him, so a person with capacity may have a copy on request. The original, including the refusal entry, stays on file.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CClinician to clinicianA transfer between professionals feels tidier and more secure.He is entitled to his own record, and he decides where it goes next.
ARefuse until he agreesIt reads as leverage that might yet protect his sight.Access to a record is not conditional on accepting treatment.
5 · DDefinitive testing offered here, then review

A positive screen is not a diagnosis, and four in five are false, so fields and imaging come before any drop. Offering the tests he will accept keeps him in view and dates the next decision.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EStart a prostaglandin25 mmHg with a 0.7 cup looks like enough to justify treating now.Treating on a screen commits him to therapy for an unmade diagnosis.
ARescreen in a yearA year is a reasonable interval for following a glaucoma suspect.He holds an untested positive result and a family history, not a clearance.
If-then rules from this caseIf a screen is positive, work out the positive predictive value before acting on it. If a person with capacity refuses, record what was explained, what was refused, and the review offered.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-07 · 100
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 7101

Case S1-07 · why

1 · CPosterior capsule opacification

A wrinkled pearly membrane with Elschnig pearls behind a centred implant accounts for glare and reduced acuity years after surgery. A normal OCT and stable refraction exclude the alternatives.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BPseudophakic CMOIt is the classic late cause of blur after cataract surgery.OCT shows a normal macula, and CMO peaks in months, not two years.
ARefractive changeNew spectacles are the simplest explanation for gradual blur.Refraction is unchanged from one month after his surgery.
2 · DNd:YAG posterior capsulotomy

A YAG capsulotomy clears the axis in minutes without entering the eye, and his symptoms justify it. Surgery is kept for a dense fibrotic plate the laser cannot open.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CLens exchangeIt removes membrane and pearls together in one definitive operation.It trades a two-minute laser for intraocular surgery and its risks.
BAnti-glare spectaclesGlare is his main complaint, and coatings genuinely reduce it.The scatter is inside the eye. No coating reaches behind the implant.
3 · BSame-day dilated peripheral examination

A field shadow with new floaters after capsulotomy is a retinal detachment until proven otherwise, and the laser raises that risk. Only a dilated peripheral view answers the question.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CReassure about floatersDebris and floaters are genuinely common in the first days.A field defect is not debris. The periphery has to be seen today.
EMacular OCTPost-laser oedema is real and OCT is the test that finds it.OCT misses the peripheral break causing an upper field shadow.
4 · EA spike to 31 mmHg before discharge

Pressure peaks in the first hours after capsulotomy as pigment and debris load the outflow. A rise into the thirties is treated and rechecked before he goes home.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ARise to 21 mmHgIt is a rise, and any rise after laser feels like a complication.A few points of transient rise is expected and settles untreated.
BPigment, same pressureVisible pigment is exactly the mechanism of the spike.Pigment without a pressure rise needs recheck, not treatment.
5 · A, EDisclose detachment risk, record alternatives

Consent covers the material risks a patient in his position would want, detachment and pressure rise among them, and the alternatives including doing nothing. Outcomes are explained, not promised.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BRelative consents tooInvolving family feels safer for an older patient.He has capacity. The consent is his alone to give.
DGuarantee 20/20He reached 20/20 before, so promising it sounds honest.A guaranteed outcome is not consent. State the expected range.
If-then rules from this caseIf vision falls years after cataract surgery with a clear macula and stable refraction, look behind the implant. If a field shadow follows capsulotomy, dilate and examine the periphery that day.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-19 · 102
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 19103

Case S2-19 · why

1 · DAcute bacterial endophthalmitis

Pain with loss to hand movements on day 3, a hypopyon, fibrin and dense vitreous opacities is infection inside the eye. Vitreous involvement is what separates it from anterior causes.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EToxic anterior segmentIt also gives fibrin and a hypopyon after routine surgery.It starts within 24 hours, spares the vitreous, and is not painful.
BRetained lens matterRetained cortex does inflame an eye during the first week.It does not produce a hypopyon with dense vitreous opacity in hours.
2 · CTap and inject vancomycin with ceftazidime

Bacteria multiply in vitreous within hours and only intravitreal drug reaches it, so the sample and the injection happen at one sitting, today. Topical and oral routes do not reach vitreous.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DTopical, review in 12 hoursIntensive drops treat corneal infection and feel proportionate.Drops do not reach the vitreous, and 12 hours is the retina's margin.
AWait for culturesTargeted therapy after a result is sound antimicrobial practice.Therapy here is empirical. Sampling and injection go together.
3 · D, E, FSample, inject, vitrectomy if light perception

Samples are taken before drug is given, cover is broad and intravitreal, and presenting vision decides surgery: light perception only is the threshold at which vitrectomy does better.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AAmphotericin routinelyFungal endophthalmitis is devastating, so cover looks safer.Fungus is rare on day 3 and amphotericin is toxic to the retina.
B, COral only, lens outBoth look aggressive in roughly the right direction.Oral drug underdoses the vitreous, and the lens stays for now.
4 · EReinject and refer for vitrectomy

Failure to improve means the organism load has not been cleared, so drug is given again and the vitreous is removed with it. Steroid alone and further delay both cost retina.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AOral steroidInflammation is doing the visible damage inside the eye.Steroid with uncontrolled infection accelerates the loss.
CSwitch to an antifungalFailure of antibacterial therapy suggests the wrong organism.Reinjection and vitrectomy come first. Fungal onset is later and slower.
5 · AOcular surface flora through the wound

Most acute cases come from the patient's own lid and conjunctival flora entering at surgery, which is why antisepsis matters more than the choice of antibiotic. Coagulase-negative staphylococci lead.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CPropionibacteriumIt is the organism every postoperative list carries.It presents weeks to months later, indolently, with a capsular plaque.
DSterile, from fluidContaminated fluid does cause clusters after cataract surgery.Sterile syndromes appear on day 1 and spare the vitreous.
If-then rules from this caseIf an eye loses vision with pain in the first week after surgery and the vitreous is involved, tap and inject the same hour. If vision is light perception, arrange vitrectomy.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-20 · 104
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 20105

Case S2-20 · why

1 · EToxic anterior segment syndrome

Onset within 24 hours, limbus-to-limbus oedema with a fixed dilated pupil, no vitreous involvement and three eyes from one list is a toxic insult delivered into the anterior chamber.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AEndophthalmitisFibrin with a raised pressure after surgery reads as infection.Infection is painful, starts later, and takes the vitreous with it.
DSurgical decompensationA hard cataract can leave a swollen cornea on day 1.Trauma gives focal oedema, not a fixed pupil in three patients.
2 · D, E, FDay 1 onset, whole-cornea oedema, a cluster

Toxic injury is immediate, spread across the whole cornea because the insult was inside the chamber, and shared by whoever shared the exposure. Fibrin, pressure and acuity occur in both.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AFibrin in the chamberA fibrinous chamber is the sign that dominates the examination.Both diagnoses produce fibrin, so it never decides the question.
B, CPressure, acuityBoth are abnormal and both look like markers of severity.Severity is shared. Timing and distribution are not.
3 · DIntensive steroid, pressure control, same-day review

The injury is a sterile chemical one, so treatment is anti-inflammatory with pressure control and a recheck the same day. If vitreous cells appear or pain begins, treat the eye as infected.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ETap and injectMissing endophthalmitis is the costlier error, so cover it.Day 1 onset, clear vitreous, no pain, and three eyes on one list.
BAntibiotic, 48 hoursIt hedges towards infection and avoids an injection.It treats nothing here and leaves the eye unseen for 2 days.
4 · DQuarantine instruments and fluids, audit reprocessing

A cluster points at the process rather than the patient, so shared instruments, solutions and reprocessing steps are held and examined. Nothing else stops the next eye being injured.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EChange prophylaxisAdjusting antibiotics feels like the standard response to a cluster.The insult is toxic, not infective. Prophylaxis changes nothing.
BTest viscoelastic onlyViscoelastic is a real culprit, so testing it looks targeted.Testing one item leaves detergent, autoclave and solution errors in use.
5 · EResidue on reprocessed instruments

Poor cleaning of phaco tips and cannulas leaves detergent, denatured viscoelastic and endotoxin to enter the chamber. Preserved intracameral drugs and solutions of wrong pH do the same.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
APreservative-free lidocaineIntracameral drug is an established cause of this syndrome.It is preserved drug that injures. Unpreserved lidocaine is standard.
B, CIodine, lensBoth touch the eye, and iodine is toxic to endothelium at strength.Antisepsis at 5% on skin and an acrylic lens give no diffuse oedema.
If-then rules from this caseIf the anterior segment inflames within 24 hours with limbus-to-limbus oedema and a clear vitreous, treat as toxic and examine the list. If the vitreous is involved, treat as infection.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-21 · 106
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 21107

Case S2-21 · why

1 · BUGH syndrome from a sulcus implant

A single-piece haptic in the sulcus chafes the back of the iris: a transillumination defect, dispersed pigment, recurrent bleeding, cells and 32 mmHg in that eye alone, with a normal fellow eye.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACutibacterium endophthalmitisIt is also a late, low-grade, relapsing uveitis in a pseudophakic eye.No capsular plaque, and blood with an iris defect is mechanical.
DPigmentary glaucomaHeavy angle pigment and iris transillumination are its signature.That is bilateral, in young myopes, and it does not bleed.
2 · C, D, EImage the haptics, settle the eye, refer

The eye is settled medically while the cause is confirmed and the implant dealt with. Chafing is mechanical, so no drop cures it, and laser and anti-VEGF treat mechanisms not at work here.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
FProstaglandin first lineIt is the strongest single agent for a pressure of 32 mmHg.It is avoided while the chamber is inflamed and bleeding.
A, BTrabeculoplasty, anti-VEGFOne targets the pigmented angle, the other the bleeding vessels.The angle is loaded by chafe, and there are no new vessels.
3 · DAqueous suppression with a beta-blocker and a CAI

Aqueous suppression lowers pressure without stirring the eye, and 48 hours is short enough to catch a further rise. Prostaglandins and miotics worsen inflammation, and laser adds pigment load.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ELatanoprost at nightIt is the usual first choice and the most effective single drop.It aggravates active uveitis, which is what is driving this eye.
CPilocarpine with steroidMiosis pulls the iris off the haptic and outflow improves.Miotics increase inflammation and drag iris across the optic.
4 · CPigment and blood clog the meshwork

Gonioscopy shows an open angle heavy with pigment, and the chamber holds red cells. Both load the meshwork, which is why pressure rises with each bleed and falls between them.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DAngle closure by synechiaeChronic uveitis does scar the angle shut and raise pressure.Gonioscopy shows the angle open along its whole circumference.
BSteroid responseHe is likely to be given steroid, and steroid does raise pressure.The pressure was already 32 mmHg before any steroid was given.
5 · BKeep the aspirin, deal with the implant

The bleeding is mechanical, from a haptic rubbing iris vessels, so withdrawing antiplatelet cover risks stent thrombosis without stopping the bleed. The implant is the treatable part.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CStop the aspirinAntiplatelet drugs do prolong bleeding, and this eye keeps bleeding.A coronary stent makes that a cardiac risk for no ocular gain.
EAspirin, then observeBleeds have settled by themselves four times already.Pressure is 32 with each episode. Waiting costs optic nerve.
If-then rules from this caseIf a pseudophakic eye bleeds again with pigment dispersion and an iris transillumination defect, image the haptics. If pressure and bleeding recur, move the implant.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-22 · 108
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 22109

Case S2-22 · why

1 · BLate in-the-bag dislocation

Implant and contracted capsule move together, sit low and tilted, and the eye has gained +2.25 D, so the whole complex has fallen back on a failing zonule. Pseudoexfoliation explains why.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACapsule opacificationIt is the usual reason vision fades years after cataract surgery.A membrane does not tilt the optic, wobble, or shift refraction.
COut-of-the-bag dislocationBoth leave a displaced implant in an eye operated years ago.The capsule has come with it, so the haptics are still in the bag.
2 · B, C, DWobble, tilt and a hyperopic shift

Movement of implant and capsule as one unit, a tilted low position and a change in refraction all measure lost support. A capsule that has only contracted stays where the zonule holds it.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EPseudoexfoliationIt is the reason the zonule failed, so it reads as the evidence.It predicts failure. It does not show that anything has moved.
A, FIOP 22, nine yearsOne is abnormal, the other the interval that invites suspicion.Pressure reflects the meshwork, and time alone measures nothing.
3 · APlanned surgical refixation

The complex is mobile and the optic edge crosses the pupil, so acuity will not hold and full dislocation is likely. It is a planned list operation, not an emergency and not a laser.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BEmergency vitrectomyA mobile implant in a nine-year-old eye feels like an emergency.It is still in place with a flat retina. The list can be planned.
CYAG to the phimosisThe capsule has contracted, and laser does open a tight rim.Cutting a supporting rim on a loose complex hastens the drop.
4 · EFree dislocation with a pressure of 42

An implant loose in the vitreous with a pressure of 42 threatens optic nerve and retina today. The other changes are reasons to bring the planned operation forward, not to admit him.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ATwo lines over monthsFalling acuity is what the operation is for in the first place.A slow drop moves him up the list. It is not an emergency.
DEdge across the axisIt explains the double image and the poor acuity he reports.It is the same disease at the same speed, with the eye intact.
5 · DPseudoexfoliative zonular weakness

Pseudoexfoliative material on both pupil margins marks a fibrillopathy that degrades the zonule over years, and it is the commonest reason a bagged implant descends late.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ECapsule contractionThe capsule here has plainly contracted around the optic.Contraction pulls the rim in. It does not detach the zonule.
COld capsule tearA tear at surgery is the classic route to a displaced implant.That implant falls early, and his operation was uneventful.
If-then rules from this caseIf pseudophakic vision changes with head position, look for movement of the whole complex rather than a membrane. If it is mobile but still in place, plan surgery rather than admit.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-01 · 110
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 1111

Case S1-01 · why

1 · BOrbital cellulitis, ethmoid origin

Proptosis, painful restricted motility, RAPD, reduced colour and acuity, and raised IOP all place the disease behind the septum. Fever with a week of purulent rhinorrhoea names the source.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACavernous sinus thrombosisThe correct escalation of this infection, and it also gives proptosis and chemosis.Signs stay strictly unilateral, with no multiple cranial neuropathy and no V1-V2 loss.
DPreseptal cellulitisThe visible finding is a swollen lid, and preseptal disease is commoner at this age.Preseptal disease spares acuity, pupil, colour, motility and globe position.
CRhabdomyosarcomaRapid proptosis in a child is the classic teaching phrase for it.Fever and purulent sinus disease over three days fit infection, not tumour.
2 · A, D, FRAPD, red desaturation, reduced acuity

The item asks for afferent function, not for evidence of infection. An RAPD, dyschromatopsia and reduced best acuity each measure optic nerve conduction directly.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CIOP 27 mmHgIt is genuinely abnormal, and orbital pressure is what threatens the nerve.It measures orbital pressure, not conduction. Normalising it proves nothing.
B, EFever, purulent dischargeThey confirm infection and drive the diagnosis.Neither measures the eye. A febrile child can have a normal afferent system.
3 · ESame-day referral, contrast CT, IV antibiotics

Orbital cellulitis with afferent dysfunction threatens the eye and, through intracranial extension, life. Imaging, intravenous therapy and surgical availability are arranged together, today.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BIV antibiotics tomorrowIt sees that intravenous therapy is needed and feels like a fair compromise.The overnight delay is where vision goes. The decision point is the RAPD, now.
AOutpatient MRI in 48 hoursMRI is the better soft-tissue study, so it reads as the more thorough answer.Contrast CT answers today's questions faster: sinus, collection, apex, bone.
4 · ALamina papyracea, from the ethmoid

The medial orbital wall is paper-thin and perforated by vessels, which makes contiguous ethmoid spread the usual route and explains why abduction and elevation fail first.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BValveless venous spreadA real mechanism, and how mid-face infection reaches the cavernous sinus.Not the usual route from sinusitis, and it does not localise medially.
COptic canal, sphenoidSphenoid disease is dangerous and sits against the orbital apex.Rare in children, and it would show apical signs before medial restriction.
5 · AFalling function with an apical abscess

Drainage is driven by function and location, not by the presence of a collection. Falling afferent function with apical involvement is the indication, as is failure to improve on adequate therapy.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EAbscess, function improvingAn abscess on imaging feels like a surgical finding by definition.Small medial collections often resolve on antibiotics. Imaging does not decide.
CChemosis increasingIt looks like deterioration in front of you.Surface swelling with unchanged afferent function is no reason to open an orbit.
If-then rules from this caseIf lid swelling comes with any change in acuity, colour, pupil, motility or globe position, treat it as orbital until imaging says otherwise. If function falls, drain.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-13 · 112
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 13113

Case S2-13 · why

1 · DSebaceous carcinoma of the tarsus

Recurrence at one site, lash loss with destruction of the posterior margin, yellow tarsal thickening and a unilateral chronic conjunctivitis are destructive disease. The node raises the stakes.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ERecurrent chalazionTwo surgeons treated it as one, and chalazia genuinely do recur.A chalazion does not destroy lash follicles or the posterior lid margin.
CChronic blepharitisUnilateral chronic conjunctivitis with lid disease reads as blepharitis.Blepharitis is bilateral and builds no firm nodule inside the tarsus.
2 · AMadarosis with loss of margin architecture

Loss of lash follicles and of the posterior margin means the lesion is replacing tissue rather than distending it. Recurrence, colour and chronic redness each have benign explanations.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BRecurrence after surgeryThree episodes in 14 months is what prompted the referral.Incompletely drained chalazia recur too. Recurrence is not destruction.
C, EYellow, firm noduleYellow lipid and firmness feel like the signature of this tumour.Old granulation tissue is firm and yellow as well.
3 · EFull-thickness biopsy with map biopsies

The tumour sits within the tarsus and spreads inside conjunctival epithelium, so a superficial sample misses it. Mapping defines pagetoid extent, which decides how much tissue must go.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACurettage with histologyIt samples the lesion and repeats a familiar, quick procedure.Curettings miss tarsal depth and pagetoid spread, and blur the margins.
CExcision with 2 mm marginsExcising it looks decisive and treats the lesion in one visit.Margins are planned after histology and mapping, not before them.
4 · C, D, EPagetoid spread, skip lesions, lipid lost

Intraepithelial spread imitates chronic blepharoconjunctivitis, skip lesions make one negative sample falsely reassuring, and lipid dissolves in routine processing so the cells look bland.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
FEccrine originNaming a gland of the lid sounds like the pathological detail.It arises in meibomian and Zeis glands, which are sebaceous.
A, BEarly spread, lower lidBoth sound like facts that would explain a missed tumour.The upper lid is the commoner site, and nodes follow visible disease.
5 · AWide excision with margins, node assessed

Clearance is judged on controlled margins, and pagetoid disease needs conjunctival treatment as well. A palpable node is imaged and sampled because this tumour spreads through lymphatics.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BCryotherapy, mitomycinTopical treatment spares the lid and is used for conjunctival spread.They are adjuncts to excision, not treatment for an invasive tarsal mass.
EExcision, node laterThe node is small and mobile, so watching it seems reasonable.A 1 cm node with an invasive tumour is imaged and sampled now.
If-then rules from this caseIf a chalazion recurs at one site, or takes lashes or the posterior lid margin, send full-thickness tissue. If a lid carcinoma is confirmed, examine and image the nodes.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-14 · 114
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 14115

Case S2-14 · why

1 · BCongenital nasolacrimal duct obstruction

Watering from the first weeks with morning crusting, a high tear lake, delayed dye disappearance and mucoid reflux from the sac places the block low in a patent, comfortable eye.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CCongenital glaucomaEpiphora since infancy is the presentation the two conditions share.Corneal diameters of 10.5 mm, clear corneas, no photophobia or squeezing.
EDacryocystoceleMucoid reflux comes from a sac that is holding its contents.A dacryocystocele presents in the neonate as a tense bluish swelling.
2 · DNormal corneal diameter with a clear cornea

Raised pressure in infancy stretches and clouds the cornea, so normal diameters with clear corneas are hard evidence against it. The other findings show a blocked duct but exclude nothing.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ENo photophobiaPhotophobia and squeezing are the taught triad with epiphora.Early congenital glaucoma can water without obvious light aversion.
AMucoid refluxReflux proves the duct is blocked, which feels like enough.A blocked duct and a raised pressure can sit in the same eye.
3 · AMassage and hygiene, review at 12 months

Most obstructions at Hasner open during the first year, so conservative care with a review date is the default at 9 months. Antibiotic is for purulent episodes, not as a standing course.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BProbing nowIt cures the problem in one visit after 8 months of watering.At 9 months most open without anaesthesia. Probing can wait.
CContinuous antibioticMorning crusting looks infective and drops settle it briefly.It treats discharge, not obstruction, and selects resistant organisms.
4 · B, C, DDacryocystitis, tense sac, persistence

Infection of the sac, a distended sac in a neonate, which can also obstruct the nose, and persistence past the age at which spontaneous opening stops all move the child to surgical care.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
E, FCrusting, windThey are the symptoms the parents most want stopped.Both are typical of simple obstruction that still resolves on its own.
AMucoid refluxReflux from the sac sounds like a sac that needs an operation.It is expected in ordinary obstruction and does not change the timing.
5 · DProbing and irrigation under anaesthesia

Spontaneous opening becomes unlikely after the first year, and probing at this age succeeds as a single procedure in most children. Intubation and bypass surgery are for failures.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EWait until 24 monthsConservative care was the right answer for the whole first year.The resolution curve flattens after 12 months. Waiting buys nothing.
BPrimary bypass surgeryIt is the definitive operation, so it looks like the thorough answer.It is reserved for failed probing and intubation at this age.
If-then rules from this caseIf an infant waters with clear corneas of normal diameter, treat as duct obstruction and review at 12 months. If the sac becomes infected or tensely distended, refer at once.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-33 · 116
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 33117

Case S1-33 · why

1 · C+6.25 D add, working at 16 cm

Kestenbaum: the starting add is the reciprocal of the acuity fraction, so 125/20 = 6.25 D. Its focal distance is 1/6.25 = 0.16 m, and the page has to be held there.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
E+10.00 D at 10 cmIt is the familiar answer, but for 20/200 rather than 20/125.The reciprocal of 125/20 is 6.25, not 10.
A+4.00 D at 25 cmIt matches the 25 cm she already uses and feels comfortable.+4.00 magnifies too little to bring 1.6 M performance down to 1 M.
2 · DScotoma to the right of fixation

Letter acuity measures one target at a time. A scotoma on the side the eye is moving into hides the next word, so saccades land in the blind area and the line is lost.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BAcuity reserveToo small a reserve does slow reading, and hers is modest.Reserve limits speed and endurance, not the ability to track a line.
APoor illuminationOne ceiling light is genuinely inadequate for atrophy.Light raises contrast and comfort, but it cannot fill a scotoma.
3 · A, BTask lighting at the page, eccentric viewing

Atrophy needs light on the page, and a scotoma over the incoming text needs a new preferred locus. Both act on what limits her, unlike a coating, a tint, or a lens held out of focus.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CYellow tintTints are popular and can cut glare on a bright page.They also cut light reaching a retina that needs more of it.
D3x hand magnifier3x is close to the magnification the arithmetic asks for.Held at arm's length it is out of focus, and her tremor moves it.
4 · BIlluminated stand magnifier

A stand magnifier holds the lens at its focal distance for her, which a resting tremor cannot do with a hand-held lens, and its own light supplies what one ceiling fitting does not.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CHand magnifierIt is portable, cheap, and the usual first device offered.A resting tremor moves the lens off its focal distance continuously.
D+6.25 D at 25 cmThe add is the one the arithmetic gives, and the habit is hers.A +6.25 D lens focuses at 16 cm. At 25 cm the print is still blurred.
5 · AState the standard, document, name the duty to notify

The consultation is where this is settled: a plain statement that she is below the standard, the advice not to drive, her response, and who must notify. The record of that talk is the evidence.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BNotify without telling herReporting duties exist and other road users are genuinely at risk.Notification never precedes telling the patient, whoever has to do it.
CDaylight, familiar routesIt sounds proportionate and preserves her independence.An acuity below the standard is below it in daylight on a known road.
If-then rules from this caseIf letter acuity is better than reading performance, look for the scotoma and the lighting before adding magnification. If acuity is below the driving standard, say so in the consultation.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-06 · 118
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 6119

Case S2-06 · why

1 · ERod-cone dystrophy, RP spectrum

Night blindness from his teens, a 12° field, non-detectable rod responses with cones still working, and mid-peripheral spicules with waxy pallor are the rod-cone sequence.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACone-rod dystrophyAcuity is already 20/60, so the cones are plainly involved too.Cone-rod disease takes acuity and colour first, and spares the field.
BChoroideremiaIt gives night blindness and mid-peripheral change in a man.Its atrophy bares choroidal vessels and white sclera, not spicules.
2 · E+8.00 D, reading at 12.5 cm

He needs twice the magnification 25 cm gives: 1 / 0.25 = 4.00 D, and 4.00 × 2 = 8.00 D. The working distance follows, since 1 / 8 = 0.125 m, so the page comes to 12.5 cm.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
B+4.00 D at 25 cmIt is the dioptric equivalent of the distance he already uses.It reproduces 2 M print. Halving print size needs double the power.
D+6.00 D at 16.7 cmIt is more power than he has now, so the print does get smaller.1.5 times the power reaches 1.3 M print. The figure needed is 8.00 D.
3 · A, E, FMobility, a +8.00 add with light, genetics

The field costs him mobility, the acuity costs him print, and the diagnosis carries inheritance. Each is met directly: training, an add with light, and testing that gates future therapy.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
C40 Δ Fresnel prismsPeripheral prisms genuinely help field loss, in hemianopia.They shift a field rather than widen a 12° ring, and they blur it.
DReversed 3x telescopeMinification does put more of the room inside a 12° field.It cuts 20/60 to 20/180. It is a spotting device, not worn full time.
4 · BRods die first, and rods are peripheral

In rod-cone disease the rods degenerate first, and rod density peaks about 20° from fixation. Scotopic vision and the mid-peripheral field go long before the cone-rich fovea.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ABipolar transmissionIt is the mechanism of congenital stationary night blindness.That is stationary from birth, with a normal fundus and full field.
ESlow cone regenerationDark adaptation is slow, so a pigment cycle fault fits loosely.Cones do not carry scotopic vision, and his cone responses remain.
5 · EMeasure, tell him, advise stopping, report

A 12° field is far under 110°, so the finding is not borderline. The duty is to measure, to tell him plainly, to advise against driving, and to record and report as the state requires.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BOn-road assessment firstFunctional testing sounds fairer than a number on a chart.No road test rewrites a 12° field against a 110° standard.
CA daylight restrictionIt keeps some independence, and dusk is when he struggles most.The field fails in daylight too, and only the state sets restrictions.
If-then rules from this caseIf night blindness comes years before the acuity falls, test the field and the rods, not just the chart. If the field is under the driving standard, record it, say so, and report as required.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-30 · 120
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 30121

Case S1-30 · why

1 · C3.2 Δ net, base up before the right eye

Prism equals decentration in centimetres times lens power. Right 0.8 cm x 5.00 D gives 4.0 Δ base up, left 0.8 cm x 1.00 D gives 0.8 Δ base up, so the net imbalance is 3.2 Δ.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
D4.0 ΔIt is the prism induced by the right lens, correctly calculated.The left lens induces 0.8 Δ the same way, and only the difference counts.
E4.8 ΔAdding the two prisms feels right when both eyes are affected.Both bases are up, so the two prisms subtract rather than add.
2 · CInduced imbalance beats the fusion range

Vertical fusional amplitude is small, 2 Δ here, and the reading position generates 3.2 Δ. The deviation appears only in downgaze, which is why her distance vision is comfortable.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DDecompensating phoriaNew spectacles often unmask a phoria, and the timing fits exactly.The distance cover test is orthophoric and the symptom is position dependent.
ESuperior oblique palsyVertical diplopia worst on reading is the palsy's textbook symptom.No torsion, no gaze or tilt pattern, and it began with the new lenses.
3 · A, E, FNear pair, slab-off, contact lenses

The imbalance is decentration multiplied by unequal power, so each fix removes one of the two: centre the lenses where she looks, cancel the difference, or move the correction to the cornea.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BHigher fitting crossAdjusting the fitting height does sound like the lever to pull.Raising the centres increases the drop in downgaze and the imbalance with it.
DPolycarbonate lensesThinner lighter lenses do improve tolerance of an anisometropic Rx.Material changes cosmetics and weight, never the induced prism.
4 · CDedicated near pair, centres lowered

Centring each lens where she looks removes the decentration, and with it the induced prism, without touching her distance correction or her acuity in either eye.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EReduce the right powerEqualising the powers does remove the imbalance arithmetically.It leaves the right eye 1.00 D under-corrected and blurred at all distances.
DBase-down in the rightPrescribing the opposing prism looks like a direct correction.It corrects downgaze and breaks fusion at distance, where she is comfortable.
5 · DCalculate induced prism at the near position

With 4.00 D of anisometropia, any vertical drop below the optical centres creates imbalance, so the calculation belongs at the design stage, before the lenses are cut and glazed.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EVertex distanceVertex distance matters at these powers and is easy to check.It changes effective power, not the prism induced by decentration.
AIPD to 1 mmCentration error is the problem, so a PD check feels right.Horizontal centration is correct here, and the drop is vertical.
If-then rules from this caseIf lens powers differ by more than 1.00 D, compute the induced prism at the reading position before dispensing. If it exceeds the vertical fusion range, centre a separate near pair.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-31 · 122
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 31123

Case S1-31 · why

1 · COver-minus from a shorter dispensed vertex

A minus lens moved closer to the eye gains effective minus power, so -12.00 specified at 14 mm is too strong at 9 mm. Sliding the frame out restores the refracting vertex and the acuity.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DPantoscopic tiltTilt does induce cylinder, and it grows fast with lens power.Tilt-induced blur does not clear when the frame slides away from the eye.
BAniseikoniaMagnification effects are real at -12.00 and cause asthenopia.Both eyes carry the same power, so the two retinal images match in size.
2 · D-11.25 DS at the 9 mm vertex

The lens sits 5 mm closer, so it needs less minus: F = -12.00 / (1 + 0.005 × 12.00) = -11.32 D, which is -11.25 to the nearest quarter dioptre.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
A-13.00 DSIt assumes a shorter vertex needs more minus, the commoner reflex.Closer to the eye a minus lens gains effective power, so it needs less.
E-10.25 DSIt is the right arithmetic for the corneal plane, a contact lens power.The lens plane here is 9 mm, not 0 mm, so only 5 mm of the shift applies.
3 · D, EClearer with the frame out, plus accommodative ache

Under-correction does not improve as the lens moves out, and it does not force accommodation. Both features here are the signature of too much minus in front of an eye working to clear it.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
F14 mm refracting vertexIt is where the error starts, so it feels like the key finding.It gives the mechanism, not the direction the error runs in.
AAcuity 20/25Reduced acuity is the presenting complaint and is genuinely abnormal.Over-minus and under-correction both blur distance by a similar amount.
4 · DRemake at -11.25 DS for the fitted vertex

The frame was measured correctly, the power was not. Respecifying the power for the plane it will be worn at keeps the short vertex, which a high myope wants for field and magnification.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EHold the frame at 14 mmIt restores the conditions the refraction was measured under.It gives away the field and the reduced minification a short vertex buys.
BReading additionThe ache is accommodative, and an add relieves accommodative strain.It leaves the distance power wrong and asks a 24-year-old to stop accommodating.
5 · D-10.25 D at the corneal plane

At the cornea the whole 14 mm applies: F = -12.00 / (1 + 0.014 × 12.00) = -10.27 D. Order -10.25 and check the over-refraction on the eye.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
A-12.00 DCopying the spectacle power is quick and works at low powers.At -12.00 it over-minuses by about 1.75 D at the corneal plane.
B-11.25 DIt is the right compensation, but for the spectacle plane at 9 mm.A contact lens sits at the cornea, so the full 14 mm shift applies.
If-then rules from this caseIf a high power is dispensed at a vertex other than the one it was refracted at, respecify the power for the plane it will be worn at. If the lens moves closer, minus needs less minus.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-03 · 124
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 3125

Case S2-03 · why

1 · EVertical imbalance from anisometropia

Reading 10 mm below the optical centres of lenses that differ by 2.00 D induces unequal base-up prism. That is why the two lines separate at near while distance stays comfortable.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ADecompensating hyperphoriaThere is a measured 3 Δ hyperphoria at near to point at.It appears in these lenses at the reading point, and not without them.
CA narrow corridorCorridor width is the usual complaint in a first progressive.A narrow corridor blurs and swims. It does not split print vertically.
2 · C2.0 Δ of vertical imbalance at 40 cm

Prentice: prism equals decentration in cm times power. Right 1.0 × 1.25 = 1.25 Δ base up, left 1.0 × 3.25 = 3.25 Δ base up, so the imbalance is 2.0 Δ.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
D3.25 ΔIt is the left eye's prism, the larger of the two figures.The symptom comes from the difference between the eyes, not one lens.
E4.5 ΔIt adds the two prisms instead of subtracting them.Both are base up, so they partly cancel: 3.25 - 1.25 = 2.0 Δ.
3 · B, C, DSlab-off right, separate readers, short corridor

The imbalance is proportional to the power difference and to how far below the centres she reads. Slab-off cancels it in the reading zone, readers move the centres, a short corridor cuts the drop.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EA deeper frameMore lens below the centre sounds like more room to read in.Reading further below the centres multiplies the prism, not reduces it.
A2 Δ base outThe magnitude is right, and prism is the right family of answer.The imbalance is vertical. Horizontal prism does nothing to it.
4 · BCentration and heights, in the frame as worn

A height error of 3 mm changes the induced imbalance by 0.6 Δ, since 0.3 cm × 2.00 D = 0.6 Δ. The fit is verified as worn before a slab-off is ordered on the wrong lens.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DRe-measure the additionThe add is the other new number in a first progressive, and it is high.A wrong add blurs at 40 cm. It cannot separate print vertically.
CCorneal topographyIrregular astigmatism does cause monocular doubling of print.Her doubling is binocular: it clears when either eye is covered.
5 · CDissimilar segments, round top on the left

Reading above the optical centre of a round top adds base-down prism in front of the more plus left eye, offsetting its excess base up. A flat top centre sits near the reading point and adds little.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ARound top on the rightIt is the right technique, applied to the other eye of the pair.Base down is needed where the base up is largest, in the left lens.
DMatched flat tops, raisedRaising one segment does move where she reads in that lens.The distance powers still differ, so the imbalance survives the change.
If-then rules from this caseIf anisometropia passes 1.50 D, work out the Prentice prism at the reading point before dispensing. If the imbalance passes about 1.5 Δ, plan slab-off or separate readers.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-20 · 126
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 20127

Case S1-20 · why

1 · CDemyelinating retrobulbar optic neuritis

A young woman with subacute unilateral loss, pain on movement, dyschromatopsia, an RAPD and a normal disc has retrobulbar inflammation. The earlier leg symptom points to demyelination.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DAnterior ischaemicIt is the commonest optic neuropathy and shares the RAPD and colour loss.It needs a swollen disc and it does not hurt on eye movement.
EApical compressionCompression also spares the disc early and causes progressive loss.Compression progresses over months, without pain on movement.
2 · APain on movement with a normal disc

An RAPD, reduced acuity and dyschromatopsia occur in both, so neither separates them. Pain on movement with a flat disc in a woman of 29 is specific to inflammation of the nerve behind the globe.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BAfferent pupil defectIt is the sign that proves the nerve is at fault, so it feels decisive.Any unilateral optic neuropathy gives it. It localises without discriminating.
DIshihara 4 of 14Colour loss is taught as the marker of optic neuritis.Ischaemic neuropathy desaturates colour in proportion to acuity too.
3 · DContrast MRI, then neurology

Imaging counts the lesions that set the risk of multiple sclerosis and excludes compression, and that result drives disease modifying treatment. Steroids do not change the final acuity.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ESteroids firstTreating the inflammation immediately feels like the active choice.Steroids only shorten the attack, and imaging must come before immunotherapy.
AOral prednisoloneIt avoids admission and appears to give the same anti-inflammatory effect.Standard oral dosing alone was associated with more recurrent attacks.
4 · C, DRecovery expected, steroids only speed it

The natural history is good, with most eyes back near normal acuity within three months, and steroid benefit is confined to the speed of that recovery. Residual colour and contrast loss is common.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AColour fully recoversAcuity recovers so well that colour is assumed to follow it.Colour and contrast deficits persist in many eyes with 20/20 acuity.
BPoor acuity, poor outcomePresenting acuity predicts outcome in most other optic neuropathies.Even eyes at counting fingers usually recover substantially here.
5 · ETraction on the inflamed nerve sheath

The superior and medial rectus origins share the dural sheath of the nerve at the apex, so every movement pulls on an inflamed, swollen sheath. This is why the pain is worse on adduction and elevation.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACiliary artery ischaemiaIt is the mechanism of the disease this case must be separated from.Ischaemic neuropathy is painless, and the disc here is flat and normal.
CRaised orbital pressureOrbital congestion genuinely causes ache worsened by movement.There is no proptosis, no chemosis and no restriction of movement.
If-then rules from this caseIf a young adult loses vision in one eye with pain on movement and a normal disc, image the brain and orbits before reaching for steroids. If the disc is swollen instead, rethink the diagnosis.

Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 21129

Case S1-21 · why

1 · BArteritic AION from giant cell arteritis

Chalky pallid oedema, vision at counting fingers and an RAPD point to arteritic infarction rather than a crowded-disc event. Jaw ache, scalp tenderness, ESR 78 and CRP 42 name the vasculitis.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ANon-arteritic AIONCommoner, and it also gives sudden painless loss with disc oedema.Oedema here is chalky and pallid, with systemic symptoms and an ESR of 78.
CRetinal artery occlusionSudden profound loss in an elderly arteritic patient fits an arterial event.No retinal opacification and no cherry-red spot; the lesion is a pallid swollen disc.
2 · C, D, EJaw claudication, pallid oedema, raised markers

Both entities give sudden painless loss with an RAPD and disc oedema. Jaw claudication, chalky pallor of the swelling and raised ESR and CRP are the arteritic markers.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
FSmall cup in fellow eyeA crowded disc is a real risk marker, so it reads as supporting evidence.A small cup argues for the non-arteritic form, not against it.
A, BRAPD, painless onsetBoth are present and both are abnormal, so they read as diagnostic.Both occur in either form, so neither discriminates.
3 · BSteroid now, biopsy within two weeks

Sight in the fellow eye is what is at risk and it can go within days. Steroid starts on clinical suspicion with an ESR of 78, and biopsy stays positive for one to two weeks after treatment begins.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CWait for biopsyConfirming before committing an elderly patient to steroid feels careful.The fellow eye can infarct while waiting, and biopsy yield survives early steroid.
DPrednisolone 20 mgIt treats, and a lower dose limits harm in a 74-year-old.Established visual loss needs high dose; 20 mg does not protect the other eye.
4 · BShort posterior ciliary artery occlusion

The laminar and prelaminar nerve is fed by the short posterior ciliary arteries. Granulomatous inflammation of these medium-sized vessels infarcts that watershed and gives pallid swelling.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CCentral retinal veinVein occlusion also swells the disc and drops vision suddenly.No dilated tortuous veins and no scattered retinal haemorrhages.
ACarotid stenosisOcular ischaemic syndrome is real and she has vascular risk.It gives retinal signs and gradual change, not chalky disc pallor.
5 · DFellow eye symptoms with rising CRP

New obscurations in the uninvolved eye with a rising CRP mean active vasculitis and a second eye at risk. Steroid hyperglycaemia is managed alongside, not by cutting the dose.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EGlucose 9.2A real steroid effect that needs treatment and looks like a reason to taper.It calls for glycaemic control, not less steroid while sight is at risk.
BESR 40, jaw acheA falling ESR reads as control, so the residual ache seems minor.Symptoms hold the taper where it is, but no visual threat means no escalation.
If-then rules from this caseIf disc oedema is pallid, or comes with jaw claudication or scalp tenderness, start steroid before the biopsy. If the fellow eye reports obscurations, escalate the same day.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-22 · 130
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 22131

Case S1-22 · why

1 · CPapilloedema, raised intracranial pressure

Positional headache, pulsatile tinnitus, obscurations and bilateral oedema with no venous pulsation are pressure signs. Preserved acuity with a sixth nerve deficit fits pressure, not neuritis.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DBilateral optic neuritisA young woman with swollen discs and headache is the neuritis stereotype.Acuity is 20/20 with no RAPD and no pain on eye movement.
AMalignant hypertensionIt swells both discs and is the emergency nobody wants to miss.BP is 128/78, with no arteriolar narrowing and no exudates.
2 · B, C, DMRI with venography, LP after imaging, stop the drug

Pressure has to be measured and secondary causes excluded before it is called idiopathic. Venous sinus thrombosis is the dangerous mimic, and tetracyclines are a recognised trigger.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ESteroid taperSteroid does lower pressure briefly and the discs look inflamed.It masks the picture, rebounds on withdrawal, and the weight gain worsens the disease.
APrism for diplopiaThe diplopia is real and prism is the optometric reflex.The sixth nerve deficit resolves with the pressure, so prism chases a moving target.
3 · ASixth nerve stretch from raised pressure

A raised pressure gradient displaces the brainstem downward and tethers the sixth nerve where it crosses the petrous ridge. That makes it a false localising sign rather than a second disease.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BSixth nerve nucleusA nuclear lesion is the neat anatomical answer for an abduction deficit.Nuclear lesions give a gaze palsy, and there is no gaze or facial sign here.
CMedial rectus restrictionThyroid myopathy limits abduction and is common in young women.Nothing suggests restriction, and swollen discs with obscurations point intracranially.
4 · BFalling acuity with progressive field loss

Function decides. Loss of acuity and field over days is fulminant disease and needs shunting or optic nerve sheath fenestration, whereas headache and tinnitus alone do not.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CHeadache at two weeksThe symptom that brought her in has not improved, so treatment looks failed.Headache lags and has other causes, so it is not the surgical trigger.
DDiscs unchangedPersistent oedema on examination reads as uncontrolled pressure.Oedema resolves slowly. Stable fields and acuity mean the nerve is coping.
5 · CAutofluorescent lumpy margin, vessels clear

Drusen elevate the disc without swelling the nerve fibre layer, so crossing vessels stay visible and the calcified bodies autofluoresce. Absent venous pulsation and a small cup occur in both.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DNo venous pulsationIts absence is taught as the sign of raised pressure.It is absent in a fifth of normal eyes, so it cannot separate the two.
APeripapillary bleedHaemorrhage looks like the active and dangerous option.Drusen bleed too, from the same crowded disc margin.
If-then rules from this caseIf both discs are swollen with normal acuity, measure the pressure and image the venous sinuses before calling it idiopathic. If acuity or field falls over days, refer for decompression.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-23 · 132
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 23133

Case S1-23 · why

1 · AAneurysmal pupil-involving third nerve palsy

A complete external palsy with a 6 mm sluggish pupil and abrupt severe pain is compression until imaging proves otherwise. A posterior communicating artery aneurysm is what must be excluded today.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BMicrovascular palsyHe is 58 and hypertensive, which is the vasculopathic profile exactly.The pupil is 6 mm and sluggish, and the glucose is 6.1.
COcular myastheniaPtosis with diplopia and no visual loss is the myasthenic pattern.The pupil is involved and the deficit does not fatigue on upgaze.
2 · CSame-day CT angiography, neurosurgery

A pupil-involving palsy with pain is a warning leak until proven otherwise, and the rupture risk is measured in hours. CT angiography is fast, available and sensitive for aneurysms of this size.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DMRI within a weekMRI is the better parenchymal study and it does get imaging done.A week is longer than the interval in which these aneurysms rupture.
EObserve four weeksMicrovascular palsies do recover in four to twelve weeks.Observation belongs to pupil-sparing palsies, and only after imaging.
3 · A, B, FPupil involvement, down and out, no fatigue

Myasthenia never touches the pupil and it fatigues, while myopathy spares the pupil and ignores nerve territory. A fixed down and out eye with a sluggish pupil is a nerve trunk lesion.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CPtosis 5 mmPtosis is the headline sign and it is severe here.Levator weakness occurs in nerve, junction and muscle disease alike.
EPain behind the eyePain suggests compression, so it feels like a localiser.Pain tells you the cause is urgent, not where the lesion sits.
4 · CSuperficial pupillomotor fibres

The parasympathetic fibres travel on the dorsomedial surface of the nerve, fed by pial vessels. Extrinsic compression reaches them first, while microvascular disease infarcts the core.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AVasa nervorum ischaemiaIt is the real mechanism of the vasculopathic palsy that spares the pupil.Ischaemia hits the central fibres, which is why the pupil is spared.
DCiliary ganglion insideThe ganglion does sit close to the inferior division route.It lies in the orbit behind the globe, not within the nerve trunk.
5 · DRisk review, prism or occlusion, six-week check

With imaging clear, a vasculopathic palsy is the working diagnosis and most recover by twelve weeks. Relief of diplopia is temporary while the angle changes, and the blood pressure is the treatable part.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ASurgery at six weeksThe deviation is disabling and surgery is definitive.Wait at least six months for a stable measurement; most of this recovers.
BHigh-dose steroidSteroid is the reflex when a palsy fails to recover quickly.No inflammatory markers, no orbital signs, and it worsens his risk profile.
If-then rules from this caseIf ptosis and an ocular motor deficit come with a dilated sluggish pupil, image the vessels the same day. If the pupil is spared in a vasculopath, imaging still comes before observation.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S1-24 · 134
Session 1 / Teaching key, Case 24135

Case S1-24 · why

1 · ERight Horner syndrome, preganglionic

Ptosis with a raised lower lid, a smaller pupil in the dark and dilation lag are sympathetic denervation. Neck pain, hoarseness and cough put the lesion on the preganglionic run through the chest.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
APhysiological anisocoriaA fifth of people have it, and lid laxity at 52 is ordinary.Physiological anisocoria is equal in light and dark and has no dilation lag.
CPartial third palsyPtosis plus anisocoria is the third nerve pattern.The wrong pupil is small here, and motility is full.
2 · A, B, FDark anisocoria, dilation lag, raised lower lid

Sympathetic denervation weakens the dilator and the Muller muscle, so the deficit shows in the dark, dilation lags and the lower lid rises. Upper lid ptosis on its own has many causes.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
CUpper lid 2 mm lowerIt is the sign that brought him in and it is on the affected side.Aponeurotic and mechanical ptosis do the same thing without pupil signs.
D, E20/20, full motilityNormal findings feel reassuring and therefore relevant.They exclude other disease but they localise nothing.
3 · AApraclonidine 0.5%, reversal confirms

Denervation supersensitivity of the dilator lets a weak alpha agonist reverse the anisocoria: the small pupil dilates and the ptosis lifts. Hydroxyamphetamine localises, but only after confirmation.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DHydroxyamphetamineIt is the classic Horner drop and it does separate the levels.It answers where, not whether, and it cannot be used on the same day.
BPilocarpine 0.125%Dilute pilocarpine is a supersensitivity test, so the logic feels right.That test is for a tonic pupil, where the sphincter is denervated.
4 · CNeck and chest apex imaging with carotid study

The sympathetic chain runs from hypothalamus to orbit, and a hoarse voice with neck pain in a smoker points at the apex and the carotid. Imaging has to cover the whole preganglionic route.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DBrain MRI aloneCentral lesions do cause Horner syndrome and MRI is the neuro study.The clues sit in the neck and chest, and a brain study misses both.
BNo imagingIsolated Horner can be benign, and the signs are unchanged for weeks.Hoarseness, neck pain and a smoking history make observation unsafe.
5 · CAbrupt onset with neck pain and tinnitus

Dissection presents with sudden neck or facial pain, Horner syndrome within hours and pulsatile tinnitus, and it can embolise within days. Hoarseness and weight loss point instead to the apex.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BWeight loss, haemoptysisIt is the most alarming option and it does signal serious disease.It points at an apical tumour, which is urgent but is not a dissection.
ALonger dilation lagA bigger number looks like a worse lesion.Lag grades the denervation, not the cause or the level.
If-then rules from this caseIf anisocoria is greater in the dark and the pupil dilates late, call it sympathetic and confirm with apraclonidine. If the voice or the neck is involved, image the chest apex.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-11 · 136
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 11137

Case S2-11 · why

1 · EEthambutol toxic optic neuropathy

Symmetric painless loss over weeks, dense red-green failure and matched central scotomas, on 23 mg/kg with an eGFR of 42, is dose-related toxicity. Flat discs place it behind the globe.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ABilateral NAIONAge and vascular risk make ischaemia the reflex answer in an older man.Ischaemia is sudden, usually sequential, and swells the disc it damages.
BChiasmal compressionBilateral field loss with normal discs does point towards a chiasm.Compression gives temporal loss and asymmetry, not matched central scotomas.
2 · A, B, CColour loss, matched scotomas, no RAPD

Dyschromatopsia, symmetric central scotomas and an absent RAPD in eyes that both see 20/60 or worse localise to the optic nerves and say the two nerves are equally affected.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
D, EDrug and doseThey name the cause and are the reason the drug has to stop.Exposure and dose explain why the nerves are failing, not where.
FAcuity down in both eyesIt is the abnormality the patient actually came with.Reduced acuity grades severity and localises nothing at all.
3 · CStop ethambutol today, through the TB team

Toxicity is dose and duration dependent, and recovery depends on how early the drug stops. The TB service substitutes cover the same day so the regimen is not left short.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DDose reduction15 mg/kg is the low-risk dose, so it reads as the safe compromise.Risk is low at that dose in intact nerves. This nerve is already failing.
EStop all four drugsIt removes every possible culprit in one decision.Only ethambutol needs to go. Losing cover invites resistant disease.
4 · CWarn of further decline, review at 6 weeks

Loss from ethambutol often progresses for 1 to 3 months after withdrawal, and recovery, when it comes, takes 6 months or longer. Early review records the floor and catches other causes.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ERestart at a lower doseThe drug is useful and the dose was high, so a smaller dose feels safe.Rechallenge after toxicity risks permanent loss. Substitute instead.
DNormal within 2 weeksRemoving a cause usually stops a disease, so quick recovery sounds right.Decline for weeks after stopping is typical, and recovery is slow.
5 · DMitochondrial failure in papillomacular axons

Ethambutol chelates copper and zinc and impairs complex IV. The small, heavily loaded axons of the papillomacular bundle fail first, which produces central scotomas and red-green loss.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ACiliary artery infarctionIt is the mechanism behind the ischaemic neuropathy on this list.Infarction is abrupt, usually unilateral, and swells the disc.
CMagnocellular lossNaming a ganglion cell population sounds precise and mechanistic.Toxicity hits small parvocellular axons, which is why colour goes first.
If-then rules from this caseIf colour and central vision fall in both eyes on ethambutol, stop the drug the same day. If the loss is asymmetric or a disc swells, look for a second cause.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-28 · 138
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 28139

Case S2-28 · why

1 · CThe optic chiasm

Loss of both temporal fields, stopping at the vertical midline, can only come from nasal retinal fibres, and they cross in one place. Amenorrhoea with galactorrhoea names what sits below.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DLeft optic nerveOne eye is a line worse, which invites a lesion on that side.A nerve lesion takes the whole field of one eye, not two halves.
ERight optic tractA tract lesion is retrochiasmal and splits at the midline too.That gives a left homonymous defect, not both temporal halves.
2 · BA one-sided afferent pupillary defect

Asymmetry of pupillary input means one optic nerve conducts worse than the other, which is prechiasmal. Colour, acuity and disc pallor all fall with chiasmal compression as well.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DBilateral temporal pallorPallor points at the optic nerve, so it reads as prechiasmal.Chronic chiasmal compression atrophies crossing fibres in both.
C, EColour, acuityBoth measure afferent function and both are abnormal here.Chiasmal compression reduces both. Neither localises in front.
3 · CMRI of the sella with endocrine bloods

MRI shows the mass, its extent and the chiasm, and hormones decide the treatment, so the two are ordered together. Compression that has grown for two years is imaged within days.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DUnenhanced CTIt is quick, available, and excludes a large mass or a bleed.It underestimates soft tissue at the sella and misses the chiasm.
ARepeat the fieldsConfirming an unfamiliar field defect feels careful.The pattern is unambiguous, and delay costs crossing fibres.
4 · CA dopamine agonist first

A prolactin of 4200 mU/L makes this a prolactinoma, and these shrink on a dopamine agonist, often with fields recovering in weeks. Surgery is for failure or sudden visual collapse.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
DSurgery firstThe chiasm is compressed, and decompression sounds mechanical.A prolactinoma shrinks medically. Surgery is the second line.
AObservationAcuity is nearly normal, so watching looks proportionate.Both temporal fields have gone already. It is progressing.
5 · D, ENasal fibres cross, inferonasal ones lowest

Nasal retina, which sees the temporal field, crosses in the chiasm, and the inferonasal bundle crosses lowest and furthest forward. That is why superior temporal loss often comes first.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ATemporal fibres crossHalf the fibres cross, so which half is easy to invert.Temporal retina stays uncrossed. Nasal retina is what crosses.
CNasal half of the fieldNasal fibres and nasal field sound like the same thing.Nasal retina sees the temporal field. Retina and field are flipped.
If-then rules from this caseIf both temporal fields are lost at the vertical midline, localise to the chiasm and image within days. If one pupil shows an afferent defect, the disease is in front of it.

Clinical Reasoning Manual · PAM/TMODCase S2-30 · 140
Session 2 / Teaching key, Case 30141

Case S2-30 · why

1 · DThe right occipital cortex

The left half of both fields has gone at the vertical midline, so the lesion is retrochiasmal on the right. A complete, congruous defect with a spared central island is occipital.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BMeyer loopA temporal lesion is retrochiasmal and gives homonymous loss.Meyer loop takes the upper quadrant, not a whole half field.
ALeft occipital cortexOccipital is the right place, and sides are easy to invert.The left cortex serves the right half of both fields.
2 · ECongruity with a spared central island

Fibres from the two eyes lie together in the cortex, so cortical defects match closely and often spare a central island. Tract lesions are incongruous and may give a pupil defect.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
ARespects the midlineThe midline rule is the first thing taught about these fields.Everything behind the chiasm respects it, the tract included.
BNormal pupilsNormal pupils feel like evidence the lesion is far back.Pupils are often normal in tract lesions too. It rarely helps.
3 · ESame-day stroke service referral

Three days from onset, in atrial fibrillation with no anticoagulation, he is at high risk of a second embolus. The pathway that investigates and prevents that starts at the stroke service.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
BAspirin and a monthAntiplatelet therapy is part of secondary prevention.Fibrillation needs anticoagulation, decided after imaging.
CPrisms and retrainingRehabilitation is what will help the field he has lost.It treats the deficit and ignores the artery about to close.
4 · D, E, FAnticoagulation, blood pressure, low vision help

The month belongs to preventing the next infarct, so rhythm and a pressure of 176/98 are treated, while reading strategies restore what a left-sided field defect takes away.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
AOcclude one eyeOcclusion helps double vision, so it sounds like a comfort measure.Both eyes lose the same half. Covering one removes nothing.
B, CWeekly fields, steroidOne tracks recovery, the other treats swelling around an infarct.Fields are repeated at months, and steroid has no role here.
5 · CStop driving, notify the authority

A homonymous hemianopia fails the visual field standard whatever the acuity. He stops driving now, the duty to declare is his, and any return is decided on a formal field test.

OptDistractorTempting becauseRuled out by
EAcuity is enoughThe acuity standard is the one most drivers know about.Field and acuity are separate standards. This one fails on field.
DWait until it is stableSome cortical field loss does recover in the first months.Recovery is judged by the authority, not by how he feels.
If-then rules from this caseIf both eyes lose the same half of the field at the vertical midline, the lesion is behind the chiasm and it is a stroke referral. Congruity with a spared centre means occipital.
